(a)
(b)
(c)
(d)
(e)
(f)
(g)
(h)

Figure 1:

Examples of tensors and tensor networks representing objects and computations in quantum physics.
(a) A pure state
\psi
is a vector, a tensor with one index.
(b) A unitary time-evolution operator is a matrix, a tensor with two indices.
(c) A density matrix is a tensor with two indices.
(d) A quantum channel is a superoperator acting on density matrices, and has four indices (two input and two output indices).
(e) Krauss decomposition of a channel, where
A_{i}
are the Krauss operators.
(f) Expectation value of an observable (operator)
O
yields a tensor-network diagram with no open indices (the result is a number).
(g) Tensor-network equation which states that
U
is a unitary,
UU^{\dagger}=1
.
The line on the right-hand side represents the identity operator.
(h) Shows a more intricate example of a computation:
We start with a density matrix
\rho
over two degrees of freedom (first degree of freedom corresponding to the left two indices), trace out the second degree of freedom, apply a unitary
U
to the first degree of freedom, perform a measurement instrument
P
whose right index represents the classical measurement outcome, apply a classically controlled unitary
C
depending on this measurement outcome, and take the expectation value of the resulting state with respect to an observable
O
.

III.2
Definition

Having reviewed tensors and tensor networks in general, let us now define quadratic tensors, a special type of tensors.

Quadratic functions —

The essential ingredient of quadratic tensors are 2nd order functions, or in short, quadratic functions.
Quadratic functions are a generalization of 2nd order polynomial functions
q:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where each instance of
\mathbb{R}
is replaced with an arbitrary abelian group.
2
2

2

So when using the term “quadratic function”, we should keep in mind that we do allow linear and constant terms.

2nd order polynomials can be characterized by the property that their third derivative vanishes, so we start by defining a general-group analogue of the derivative of functions
q:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

{mydef}

Consider two abelian groups
G
and
A
, and a function
q:G\rightarrow A
.
Define the
0
th derivative
of
q
as

q^{(0)}\in A:\qquad q^{(0)}\coloneqq q(0)\;,

(74)

the
1
st derivative
as

\displaystyle q^{(1)}:G

\displaystyle\rightarrow A

(75)

\displaystyle q^{(1)}(g)

\displaystyle\coloneqq q(g)-q^{(0)}=q(g)-q(0)\;,

the
2
nd derivative
as

\displaystyle q^{(2)}:G\times G

\displaystyle\rightarrow A

(76)

\displaystyle q^{(2)}(g_{0},g_{1})

\displaystyle\coloneqq q^{(1)}(g_{0}+g_{1})-q^{(1)}(g_{0})-q^{(1)}(g_{1})=q(g_{0}+g_{1})-q(g_{0})-q(g_{1})+q(0)\;,

and the
3
rd derivative
as

\displaystyle q^{(3)}:G\times G\times G

\displaystyle\rightarrow A

(77)

\displaystyle q^{(3)}(g_{0},g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=q^{(2)}(g_{0}+g_{1},g_{2})-q^{(2)}(g_{0},g_{2})-q^{(2)}(g_{1},g_{2})\;.

We note that each derivative is symmetric in its arguments.

After introducing derivatives, we define quadratic functions as in the
\mathbb{R}
-valued case:
A quadratic function is one whose 3rd derivative vanishes.
We also define linear functions, which are the ones whose 2nd derivative vanishes.

{mydef}

We will call a function
q:G\rightarrow A

1st order
, or
linear

3
3

3

Again, “linear” here is used in the sense of “affine linear”, as we do allow for constant terms.

function if

q^{(2)}(g_{0},g_{1})=0\quad\forall g_{0},g_{1}\in G\;,

(78)

and
2nd order
, or
quadratic
if

q^{(3)}(g_{0},g_{1},g_{2})=0\quad\forall g_{0},g_{1},g_{2}\in G\;.

(79)

We will write
F_{1}[G|A]
and
F_{2}[G|A]
for the set of linear and quadratic functions, respectively.

q^{(2)}=0
iff
q^{(1)}
is a group homomorphism from
G\rightarrow A
.
So a linear function
q=q^{(1)}+q^{(0)}
is the same as an affine group homomorphism.

q
is quadratic iff
q^{(2)}
is a bilinear form (see Definition
III.3
).
In this context, quadratic functions
q
are known in the literature as
quadratic refinements
of the bilinear form
q^{(2)}

Quadratic_refinement_nlab
.

Quadratic tensors —

Using quadratic and linear functions, we can now define quadratic tensors.
We first describe the “data” that is necessary to fully specify a quadratic tensor, and then show how the tensor entries can be obtained from this data.

{mydef}

Let
A
be some abelian group.
A
quadratic tensor data
over an abelian group
G
is either a triple
(E,\epsilon,q)
, where

•

E
is an abelian group,

•

\epsilon
is a linear function
\epsilon\in F_{1}[E|G]
called the
embedding
,

•

q
a quadratic function
q\in F_{2}[E|A]
,

or it is equal to
0
.
Let
\exp\in\hom[A|\mathbb{C}^{\times}]
be a homomorphism from
A
to the multiplicative group of complex numbers.
The
quadratic tensor
associated with a quadratic tensor data
(E,\epsilon,q)
is a vector in the vector space of complex functions over the abelian group
G
,

\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q]:G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}:\qquad\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q](g)\coloneqq\sum_{e\in E:\epsilon(e)=g}\exp(q(e))\;.

(80)

The quadratic tensor associated to
0
is
\mathcal{T}[0](g)=0\forall g
.
Two quadratic tensor data
(E,\epsilon,q)
and
(E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime})
are considered
equivalent
if they have the same associated quadratic tensor,

\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime}]=\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q]\;.

(81)

If not mentioned otherwise,
A
will be given by its canonical choice
A=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, as this is just the additive version of
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
The canonical choice for
\exp
is the isomorphism
4
4

4

For the canonical choice, we could instead use a quadratic function
q\in F_{2}[E|\mathbb{C}^{\times}]
that directly results in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
values, and get rid of
A
and the homomorphism
\exp
.
One of the main reasons for using
A
is that we prefer additive notation for abelian groups, and it would be confusing to use “
+
” for the multiplication in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
Another reason is that we will consider
A=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
with
\exp(\phi)=e^{2\pi i\phi}
at some point in Section
III.6
.

\exp((a,\phi))=e^{2\pi(a+i\phi)}\;.

(82)

This is a canonical choice since we do not gain anything by choosing a “larger”
A
.
For the canonical choice, we can split
q=(q_{a},q_{\phi})
into
q_{a}\in F_{2}[E|\mathbb{R}]
and
q_{\phi}\in F_{2}[E|\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}]
.
5
5

5

More generally, we may replace
\mathbb{C}
by an arbitrary ring
R
, and
\exp
by an arbitrary homomorphism
A\rightarrow R^{\times}
, where
R^{\times}
is the multiplicative group of
R
.
Most of the mathematical statements in this paper hold for this more general case, but
R=\mathbb{C}
with
A=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
is the choice relevant for quantum mechanics.

Physics use case —

A quadratic tensor with
n
indices is one where
G
is a cartesian product of
n
factors,
G=\bigtimes_{0\leq i<n}G_{i}
, even though the definition of a quadratic tensor is agnostic towards this decomposition.
For the physics use case, we restrict ourselves to factors that we call
elementary abelian groups
,

6
6

6

Equivalently, we could demand that
G
is a finite-dimensional abelian Lie group.

G_{i}\in\{\mathbb{Z}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\cup\{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\}\;.

(83)

Each of these elementary abelian groups corresponds to an elementary physical degree of freedom:

•

G_{i}=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
corresponds to a qubit,

•

G_{i}=\mathbb{Z}_{k}
corresponds to a
k
-dimensional qudit,

•

G_{i}=\mathbb{R}
corresponds to a continuous variable, or equivalently, an oscillator, or a bosonic mode,

•

and both
G_{i}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and
G_{i}=\mathbb{Z}
correspond to a rotor (
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
for the position basis, and
\mathbb{Z}
for the momentum basis).

Analogously, we consider
E
equal to a product of
m
elementary abelian groups.

Injective or surjective
\epsilon
—

If
E=G
and
\epsilon=\operatorname{id}
, then
\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q]
is just the element-wise exponential of the quadratic function
q
.
If
\epsilon
is not surjective, then there are elements of
G
that are not in the image of
\epsilon
, and the corresponding tensor entries are zero.
If
\epsilon
is not surjective but is injective, then
\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q]
is given by a quadratic function on some affine subspace of
G
, and zero outside of the subspace.
If
\epsilon
is not injective, then there are elements of
G
whose preimage consists of multiple elements of
E
, so the corresponding tensor entry is given by an exponential sum of
q
over the preimage.
In fact, we will see in Section
III.6
that if
E
only contains
\mathbb{Z}_{d}
or
\mathbb{R}
factors, every quadratic tensor data is equivalent to one with injective
\epsilon
.
However, for
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}
factors, the non-injective case can give rise to more general quadratic tensors.

Analytic considerations —

If
G
or
E
contain any infinite factors
\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, or
\mathbb{R}
, we should make sure that the summation over
e\in E
in Eq. (
80
) converges, or describe what to do if it does not.
We will briefly address these analytic aspects here.
In the rest of the paper, we focus on the algebraic properties of quadratic tensors, and may not always be fully explicit about analytic aspects.
First of all, if
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
contains
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
factors, then the summation in Eq. (
80
) is replaced by an integration on these factors.
If
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
contains
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{Z}
factors, then the summation or integration may not converge.
Consider
q_{a}^{(2)}|_{\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})}
, the second derivative of the quadratic function
q_{a}
restricted to the kernel of
\epsilon^{(1)}
.
As we will see in Section
III.4
,
q_{a}^{(2)}|_{\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})}
is only non-zero on the
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{Z}
factors of
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
, where it is described by an
\mathbb{R}
-valued matrix.
Now, the summation or integration is a Gaussian sum or integral if
q_{a}^{(2)}|_{\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})}<0
, and thus always converges in this case.
However, we will also consider quadratic tensor data where
q_{a}^{(2)}|_{\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})}\leq 0
, even if the summation or integration in Eq. (
80
) does not converge.
Even though these can be considered fringe cases, they occur quite often in analytical calculations in physics.
In these cases, the entries of
\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q]
are not given by a function
G\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, but a
\delta
-like distribution.
For example, consider the following quadratic tensor data over
G=\mathbb{R}
,

E=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\;,\quad\epsilon(e_{0},e_{1})=e_{1}\;,\quad q_{a}(e_{0},e_{1})=0\;,\quad q_{\phi}(e_{0},e_{1})=e_{0}e_{1}\operatorname{mod}1\;.

(84)

Even though integration in Eq. (
80
) does not converge, the associated tensor can be formally defined as a Dirac
\delta
-distribution,

\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q](g)=\sum_{e_{0},e_{1}:\epsilon(e_{0},e_{1})=g}e^{2\pi(q_{a}(e_{0},e_{1})+iq_{\phi}(e_{0},e_{1}))}=\int_{e_{0}}e^{2\pi ie_{0}g}=\delta_{g}\;.

(85)

In physics, such a tensor describes a position eigenstate
\ket{x=0}
.
Even though such a state is unphysical, it often shows up in analytical calculations.

Apart from being ill-defined if
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
contains
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{Z}
factors, we would also like to remark that the tensors resulting from Eq. (
80
) may not always be normalizable in 2-norm.
In particular, the norm is zero if the coimage of
\epsilon
contains
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
factors.
As a minimal example, consider the following quadratic tensor data over
G=\mathbb{R}
,

E=0\;,\quad\epsilon=0\;,\quad q_{a}=0\;,\quad q_{\phi}=0\;.

(86)

Here, by
E=0
, we mean the trivial 1-element group.
According to Eq. (
80
), the associated tensor entries are given by a function with zero norm,

\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q](g)=\sum_{e\in 0:0=g}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }g=0\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(87)

Further, the norm can be infinite if
q_{a}^{(2)}\leq 0
is singular.
As a simple example, consider the following quadratic tensor data over
G=\mathbb{R}
with infinite 2-norm,

E=\mathbb{R},\quad\epsilon=\operatorname{id},\quad q_{a}=0,\quad q_{\phi}=0\qquad\Rightarrow\quad\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q](g)=1\forall g\;.

(88)

In physics, such a tensor corresponds to an eigenstate of the momentum operator,
\ket{p=0}
.
Again, this state as such is unphysical, but occurs quite often in analytical calculations.

Finally, we would like to note that quadratic tensors are always normalizable (in 2-norm) and well defined if
q_{a}^{(2)}<0
.
Thus, one way to define everything rigorously is to replace each quadratic function
q
with singular
q_{a}^{(2)}\leq 0
by a converging sequence
q_{i}\xrightarrow{i\rightarrow\infty}q
with
(q_{i})_{a}^{(2)}<0
.
Then we apply all calculations to the elements in the sequence, and take the limit only at the end of the calculation (e.g., the tensor-network contraction).
The practiced physicist can instead informally keep track of the different infinities occurring in the calculation and cancel them against each other.
For example, the integral over the function in Eq. (
87
) is
1/\infty
, the integral over Eq. (
88
) is
\infty
, and their product is
1
, even though this may be hard to justify formally.
Some of the calculations in the paper will involve
|G|
which is the order of the group
G
if
G
is discrete, and the integration measure of
G
if it is continuous (i.e.,
|\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}|=1
).
For
G=\mathbb{R}
or
G=\mathbb{Z}
, the integration measure is
\infty
.

III.3
Factor decomposition of linear and quadratic functions

In this section, we show that if
G
is a direct product of
n
factors,

G=\bigtimes_{0\leq i<n}G_{i}\;,

(89)

then linear or quadratic functions over
G
can be decomposed into
O(n)
or
O(n^{2})
smaller components related to individual factors.

Factor decomposition of linear functions —

Let us start with linear functions.

{myprop}

Let
G
be a product as in Eq. (
89
).
Then there is a bijection between (1) linear functions
q\in F_{1}[G|A]
, and (2) collections of

•

a constant
q^{(0)}\in A
, and

•

for each
0\leq i<n
, a homomorphism
q^{(1)}_{i}\in\hom[G_{i}|A]
.

The collection can be obtained from
q
as

q^{(0)}\coloneqq q^{(0)}\;,\quad q^{(1)}_{i}(g)\coloneqq q^{(1)}(g|_{i})\;,

(90)

using the notation

g|_{i}\coloneqq(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{0\ldots i-1},\underbrace{g}_{i},\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{i+1\ldots n-1})\in G\;,\qquad g\in G_{i}\;.

(91)

Vice versa,
q
can be obtained from the components above as

q(g)=q^{(0)}+\sum_{0\leq i<n}q^{(1)}_{i}(g_{i})\;,

(92)

where
g_{i}
denotes the
i
th component of
g
such that

g=(g_{0},\ldots,g_{n-1})\;.

(93)

Proof.

It is easy to see that
q^{(1)}_{i}
as defined in Eq. (
90
) is indeed a homomorphism:

q^{(1)}
is a homomorphism since
q
is linear, and
q^{(1)}_{i}
is just a restriction of
q^{(1)}
to the subgroup

\underbrace{0\times\ldots}_{0\ldots i-1}\times\underbrace{G_{i}}_{i}\times\underbrace{0\times\ldots}_{i+1\ldots n-1}\subset G\;.

(94)

It is also easy to see that
q
as defined in Eq. (
92
) is indeed a linear function:
It is a sum of constants and homomorphisms evaluated on subgroups.
Finally we verify Eqs. (
92
) and (
90
) are inverses:

q^{(0)}+\sum_{0\leq i<n}q^{(1)}_{i}(g_{i})=q^{(0)}+\sum_{0\leq i<n}q^{(1)}(g_{i}|_{i})\overset{q^{(2)}=0}{=}q^{(0)}+q^{(1)}(\sum_{0\leq i<n}g_{i}|_{i})=q^{(0)}+q^{(1)}(g)=q(g)\;.

(95)

∎

The embedding
\epsilon
of a quadratic tensor is a linear function, where
E
and
G
play the role of
G
and
A
above.
Like
G
,
E
also may be a product of
m
factors

E=\bigtimes_{0\leq j<m}E_{j}\;.

(96)

We can slightly generalize the above proposition to also incorporate the decomposition of
E
.

{myprop}

Let
G
and
E
be products as in Eqs. (
89
) and (
96
).
Then there is a bijection between (1) linear functions
\epsilon\in F_{1}[E|G]
and (2) collections of

•

for each
0\leq i<n
, a constant
\epsilon_{i}^{(0)}\in G_{i}
,

•

for each
0\leq i<n
and
0\leq j<m
, a homomorphism
\epsilon^{(1)}_{ij}\in\hom[E_{j}|G_{i}]
.

The collection is obtained from
\epsilon
as

\epsilon^{(0)}_{i}\coloneqq\epsilon^{(0)}_{i}\;,\quad\epsilon^{(1)}_{ij}(e)\coloneqq\epsilon^{(1)}(e|_{j})_{i}\;.

(97)

Vice versa,
\epsilon
can be obtained from the components above via

\epsilon(e)_{i}=\epsilon^{(0)}_{i}+\sum_{0\leq j<m}\epsilon^{(1)}_{ij}(e_{j})\;.

(98)

Factor decomposition of quadratic functions —

Let us consider quadratic functions next.
The components will be normalized quadratic functions and bilinear forms as we define in the following.

{mydef}

We call a function
q:G\rightarrow A

normalized
if
q(0)=0
.
Normalized linear functions are nothing but group homomorphisms
G\rightarrow A
, so we denote their set by
\hom[G|A]
.
Inspired by this, we denote the set of normalized quadratic functions by
\hom_{2}[G|A]
.
We call a function
b:G_{0}\times G_{1}\rightarrow A
a
bilinear form
if it is a homomorphism in each component,

\begin{gathered}b(g_{0},g_{1}+g_{1}^{\prime})=b(g_{0},g_{1})+b(g_{0},g_{1}^{\prime})\;,\quad b(g_{0},0)=0\;,\\
b(g_{0}+g_{0}^{\prime},g_{1})=b(g_{0},g_{1})+b(g_{0}^{\prime},g_{1})\;,\quad b(0,g_{1})=0\;.\end{gathered}

(99)

We denote the set of bilinear forms by
\hom^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]
.

We note that the sets
F_{1}[G|A]
,
F_{2}[G|A]
,
\hom[G|A]
,
\hom_{2}[G|A]
, and
\hom^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]
form abelian groups under element-wise addition.

{myprop}

Let
G
be a product as in Eq. (
89
).
Then there is a bijection between (1) quadratic functions
q\in F_{2}[G|A]
and (2) collections of

•

a constant
q^{(0)}\in A
,

•

for every
0\leq k<n
, a normalized quadratic function
q^{(1)}_{k}\in\hom_{2}[G_{k}|A]
,

•

for every pair
0\leq k<l<n
, a bilinear form
q^{(2)}_{kl}\in\hom^{2}[G_{k},G_{l}|A]
.

The components can be obtained from
q
via

\begin{gathered}q^{(0)}\coloneqq q^{(0)}\;,\quad q^{(1)}_{k}(g)\coloneqq q^{(1)}(g|_{k})\;,\quad q^{(2)}_{kl}(g,h)\coloneqq q^{(2)}(g|_{k},h|_{l})\;.\end{gathered}

(100)

Vice versa,
q
can be reconstructed from the components as

q(g)=q^{(0)}+\sum_{0\leq k<n}q^{(1)}_{k}(g_{k})+\sum_{0\leq k<l<n}q^{(2)}_{kl}(g_{k},g_{l})\;.

(101)

Proof.

It is easy to see that
q^{(1)}_{k}
as defined in Eq. (
100
) is indeed a normalized quadratic function:
The first derivative
q^{(1)}
of
q
is a normalized quadratic function, and
q^{(1)}_{k}
is the restriction to a subgroup.
Similarly,
q^{(2)}_{kl}
is a bilinear form as it is a restriction of the bilinear form
q^{(2)}\in\hom^{2}[G,G|A]
to a subgroup in each of the two arguments.
Further, we need to show that
q
as defined in Eq. (
101
) is a quadratic function.
To this end, we note that every bilinear form
b\in\hom^{2}[X,Y|Z]
is also a (normalized) quadratic function
b\in\hom_{2}[X\times Y|Z]
, whose bilinear form
b^{(2)}
is in turn given by

b^{(2)}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=b(x+x^{\prime},y+y^{\prime})-b(x,y)-b(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=b(x,y^{\prime})+b(x^{\prime},y)\;.

(102)

So Eq. (
101
) is a sum over constants, quadratic functions, and bilinears which are also quadratic functions.
Finally, we show that Eqs. (
100
) and Eq. (
101
) are indeed inverses of another,

\begin{gathered}q^{(0)}+\sum_{0\leq k<n}q_{k}^{(1)}(g_{k})+\sum_{0\leq k<l<n}q_{kl}^{(2)}(g_{k},g_{l})\overset{\eqref{eq:2linear_decomposition}}{=}q^{(0)}+\sum_{0\leq k<n}q^{(1)}(g_{k}|_{k})+\sum_{0\leq k<l<n}q^{(2)}(g_{k}|_{k},g_{l}|_{l})\\
\overset{q^{(3)}=0}{=}q^{(0)}+\sum_{0\leq k<n}q^{(1)}(g_{k}|_{k})+\sum_{0\leq k<n}q^{(2)}_{k}(g_{k}|_{k},\sum_{k<l<n}g_{l}|_{l})\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}q^{(0)}+q^{(1)}(\sum_{0\leq k<n}g_{k}|_{k})\overset{\eqref{eq:first_derivative}}{=}q(\sum_{0\leq k<n}g_{k}|_{k})=q(g)\;.\end{gathered}

(103)

∎

Note that for some applications it is useful to also define
q^{(2)}_{kl}
for
k>l
or
k=l
.
These components are determined by the components in Proposition
III.3
, via
q^{(2)}_{kl}(g,h)=q^{(2)}(g|_{k},h|_{l})=q^{(2)}(h|_{l},g|_{k})=q^{(2)}_{lk}(h,g)
for
k>l
, and
q^{(2)}_{ll}(g,h)=q^{(2)}(g|_{l},h|_{l})=(q^{(1)}_{l})^{(2)}(g,h)
for
k=l
.

III.4
Coefficient groups

In the previous Section
III.3
, we have shown how to decompose linear and quadratic functions into smaller components related to individual product factors.
The next step is to represent each of these components by an explicit coefficient, in the case where all factors are elementary abelian groups.
That is, for each choice of elementary abelian groups
G
,
G_{0}
,
G_{1}
, and
A
, we find elementary abelian groups that we call
coefficient groups
,

\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G|A],\quad\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A],\quad\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[G|A]\;,

(104)

and which are isomorphic to the groups
\hom[G|A]
,
\hom^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]
, and
\hom_{2}[G|A]
via
coefficient isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[G|A]

\displaystyle:\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G|A]\rightarrow\hom[G|A]\;,

(105)

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]

\displaystyle:\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]\rightarrow\hom^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]\;,

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}[G|A]

\displaystyle:\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[G|A]\rightarrow\hom_{2}[G|A]\;.

Often,
G
,
G_{0}
,
G_{1}
, and
A
are clear from the context, in which case we may omit the arguments in square brackets.

Coefficient group
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}
—

Homomorphisms between elementary abelian groups are widely known.
The corresponding coefficient groups
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G|A]
are given by

\begin{tabular}[]{r|c|c|c|c}\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$\scriptstyle{G}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}}&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{\operatorname{gcd}(k,l)}$&$0$&$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$&$0$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$0$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$0$&$\mathbb{R}$&$\mathbb{R}$\end{tabular}\;,

(106)

where
\gcd(k,l)
denotes the greatest common divisor of two integers
k
and
l
.
The following table lists the corresponding coefficient isomorphisms
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[G|A](h)(g)
for
h\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G|A]
and
g\in G
,

\begin{tabular}[]{r|c|c|c|c}\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$\scriptstyle{G}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}}&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$&$\frac{l}{\operatorname{gcd}(k,l)}\overline{h}\overline{g}\operatorname{mod}l$&$0$&$\frac{1}{k}\overline{h}\overline{g}\operatorname{mod}1$&$0$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}$&$\overline{h}g\operatorname{mod}l$&$hg$&$\overline{h}g\operatorname{mod}1$&$hg$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$h\overline{g}\operatorname{mod}1$&$0$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$0$&$hg\operatorname{mod}1$&$hg$\end{tabular}\;.

(107)

Here,
\overline{\bullet}
denotes either the embedding of the set
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
into
\mathbb{Z}
as
\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
, or the embedding of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
into
\mathbb{R}
as
[0,1)
.
All products are either products in
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}
, all multiplicative inverses are in
\mathbb{R}
, and the standard embedding of
\mathbb{Z}
into
\mathbb{R}
as integers is implicit.

Coefficient group
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}
—

In order to find the coefficient group for bilinear forms
b\in\hom^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]
, we make use of an isomorphism

\displaystyle\hom^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]

\displaystyle\simeq\hom[G_{0}|\hom[G_{1}|A]]\;,

(108)

\displaystyle b(g_{0},g_{1})

\displaystyle\simeq b(g_{0})(g_{1})\;.

In other words, for every
g_{0}\in G_{0}
, the partial evaluation
b(g_{0},\bullet)
is a homomorphism from
G_{1}
to
A
.
7
7

7

This isomorphism exists for arbitrary functions
G_{0}\times G_{1}\rightarrow A
, and is known as
currying
in the context of functional programming.

Using this, we can set

\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]=\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{0}|\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{1}|A]]\;,

(109)

and

\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A](h)(g_{0},g_{1})=\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[G_{1}|A]\big(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[G_{0}|\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{1}|A]](h)(g_{0})\big)(g_{1})\;,

(110)

perhaps more readable without square brackets as

\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2}(h)(g_{0},g_{1})=\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H(h)(g_{0}))(g_{1})\;.

(111)

With this, the coefficient groups
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A]
are given by

\begin{tabular}[]{r||c|c|c|c||c|c|c|c||c|c|c|c||c|c|c|c}$A$&\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{Z}_{m}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{Z}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol &\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{R}$\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$\scriptstyle{G_{0}}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$\scriptstyle{G_{1}}$}}}&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,l,m)}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,m)}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,l)}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(l,m)}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{m}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$\mathbb{R}$\end{tabular}\;,

(112)

and the corresponding coefficient isomorphisms
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2}[G_{0},G_{1}|A](h)(g_{0},g_{1})
are

\begin{tabular}[]{r||c|c|c|c||c|c|c|c||l}$A$&\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{Z}_{m}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{Z}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&$\ldots$\\
\hline\cr\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$\scriptstyle{G_{0}}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$\scriptstyle{G_{1}}$}}}&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$&$\ldots$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$&$\overline{h}\frac{m}{\gcd(k,l,m)}\overline{g}_{0}\overline{g}_{1}$&$\overline{h}\frac{m}{\gcd(k,m)}\overline{g}_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\ldots$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{Z}$&$\overline{h}\frac{m}{\gcd(l,m)}g_{0}\overline{g}_{1}$&$\overline{h}g_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$hg_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$0$&$\ldots$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\ldots$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\ldots$\end{tabular}\\
\begin{tabular}[]{r||c|c|c|c||c|c|c|c|}$\ldots$&\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol&\lx@intercol\hfil$\mathbb{R}$\hfil\lx@intercol\vrule\lx@intercol\\
\hline\cr$\ldots$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\ldots$&$\overline{h}\frac{1}{\gcd(k,l)}\overline{g}_{0}\overline{g}_{1}$&$\overline{h}\frac{1}{k}\overline{g}_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\ldots$&$\overline{h}\frac{1}{l}g_{0}\overline{g}_{1}$&$\overline{h}g_{0}g_{1}$&$hg_{0}\overline{g}_{1}$&$hg_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$hg_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$hg_{0}g_{1}$\\
\hline\cr$\ldots$&$0$&$h\overline{g}_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\ldots$&$0$&$hg_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$hg_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$hg_{0}g_{1}$&$0$&$hg_{0}g_{1}$\end{tabular}\;.

(113)

Note that for brevity, we have omitted a “
\operatorname{mod}m
” for every entry of the
A=\mathbb{Z}_{m}
block, and a “
\operatorname{mod}1
” for every entry in the
A=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
block.
Also note that only the blocks
A=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and
A=\mathbb{R}
are directly relevant for the purpose of this section, but the other ones occur as coefficients for the embedding
\epsilon
of 3rd order tensors which we discuss in Section
.
It is easily confirmed that Eqs. (
112
) and (
113
) result from twice applying Eqs. (
106
) and (
107
) according to Eqs. (
109
) and (
110
).

Coefficient group
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.55556pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}
—

To determine the coefficient groups for normalized quadratic functions in
\hom_{2}[G|A]
, we make use of the group homomorphism

\bullet^{(2)}:\hom_{2}[G|A]\rightarrow\hom^{2}[G,G|A]\;.

(114)

We start by considering
\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})
, that is, the subgroup of bilinears
b\in\hom^{2}[G,G|A]
for which there exists
q\in\hom_{2}[G|A]
such that
q^{(2)}=b
.
It turns out that
\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})=\hom^{2}[G,G|A]
for almost all choices of elementary abelian groups
G
and
A
.
The only case where
\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})
is a proper subgroup is when
G=\mathbb{Z}_{k}
and
A=\mathbb{Z}_{l}
with both
k
and
l
even, and
k
has at least as many prime factors of
2
as
l
.

For any two
q_{0},q_{1}\in\hom_{2}[G|A]
such that
q_{0}^{(2)}=q_{1}^{(2)}=b
, we have

(q_{0}-q_{1})^{(2)}=b-b=0\qquad q_{0}-q_{1}\in\hom[G|A]\;.

(115)

Vice versa, for every
h\in\hom[G|A]
,
q_{0}+h
is a quadratic function with
(q_{0}+h)^{(2)}=b
.
So if we choose a standard
Q_{0}(b)\in\hom[G|A]
such that
Q_{0}(b)^{(2)}=b
, this identifies the preimage
(\bullet^{(2)})^{-1}(b)
with
\hom[G|A]
.
Thus, as a set,
\hom_{2}[G|A]
can be identified with the cartesian product
\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})\times\hom[G|A]
.

As a group,
\hom_{2}[G|A]
is not in general isomorphic to the direct product above, but rather to some non-trivial group extension of
\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})
by
\hom[G|A]
.
We can obtain the group structure as follows.
We find a minimal subgroup
X\subset\hom_{2}[G|A]
such that
\bullet^{(2)}
is surjective when restricted to
X
.
For example, if
\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})
is discrete and generated by a single element
b
, and take
X\subset\hom_{2}[G|A]
to be the subgroup generated by
Q_{0}(b)
.
This minimal subgroup
X
is isomorphic to a single elementary abelian group.
Then we consider the intersection of
X
with the subgroup
\hom[G|A]\subset\hom_{2}[G|A]
,
Y\coloneqq X\cap\hom[G|A]\subset\hom[G|A]
.
The quotient
\hom[G|A]/Y
is again isomorphic to an elementary abelian group.
After this,
\hom_{2}[G|A]
can be identified with a direct product of two elementary abelian groups,
\hom_{2}[G|A]\simeq X\times\hom[G|A]/Y
.
It turns out that in most cases we have
Y=0
, such that
\hom_{2}[G|A]\simeq\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})\times\hom[G|A]
is actually a direct product.
However, for some cases we find
Y=\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, and the group structure schematically looks like “
\hom_{2}[G|A]\simeq 2\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})\times\frac{1}{2}\hom[G|A]
”.
For example, for
G=\mathbb{Z}_{k}
with
k
even and
A=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, we can pick the generating bilinear form
b(g_{0},g_{1})=\frac{1}{k}\overline{g}_{0}\overline{g}_{1}\operatorname{mod}1
with corresponding quadratic function
Q_{0}(b)(g)=\frac{1}{2k}\overline{g}^{2}\operatorname{mod}1
.
The subgroup
X\subset\hom_{2}[G|A]
generated by
Q_{0}(b)
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2k}
, and it intersects with
\hom[G|A]
on a subgroup
Y\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{2}
:

(\underbrace{Q_{0}(b)+Q_{0}(b)+\ldots}_{k\text{ times}})(g)=k\frac{1}{2k}\overline{g}^{2}\operatorname{mod}1=\frac{1}{2}\overline{g}^{2}\operatorname{mod}1=\frac{1}{2}\overline{g}\operatorname{mod}1\in\hom[G|A]\;.

(116)

So we have
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}_{k}\text{ even}|\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}]=\mathbb{Z}_{2k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
.

All in all, the coefficient groups
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[G|A]
are listed in the following table,

\begin{tabular}[]{r|c|c|c|c|c}\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$\scriptstyle{G}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}}&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$ even&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$ odd&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$ even&$\mathbb{Z}_{2\gcd(k,\frac{l}{2})}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\frac{1}{2}\gcd(k,l)}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,l)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,l)}$&$0$&$\mathbb{Z}_{2k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\frac{k}{2}}$&$0$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$ odd&$\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,l)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,l)}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,l)}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\gcd(k,l)}$&$0$&$\mathbb{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{Z}_{k}$&$0$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}\times\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}\times\mathbb{Z}_{l}$&$\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0\times\mathbb{Z}$&$0$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}$&$\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}$\end{tabular}\;.

(117)

The corresponding coefficient isomorphisms
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}[G|A]((h_{2},h_{1}))(g)
for
(h_{2},h_{1})=h\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[G|A]
and
g\in G
are given by

\begin{tabular}[]{r|c|c|c|c|c}\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$\scriptstyle{G}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}}&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$ even&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$ odd&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$ even&$\scriptstyle{\frac{l}{\gcd(k,\frac{l}{2})}\frac{1}{2}\overline{h}_{2}\overline{g}^{2}-\frac{l}{\gcd(k,l)}\overline{h}_{1}\overline{g}^{2}+\frac{l}{\gcd(k,l)}\overline{h}_{1}\overline{g}}$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{l}{\gcd(k,l)}\big(\overline{(h_{2}/2)}\overline{g}^{2}+\overline{h_{1}}\overline{g}\big)}$&$0$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{k}(\frac{1}{2}\overline{h}_{2}-\overline{h}_{1})\overline{g}^{2}+\frac{1}{k}\overline{h}_{1}\overline{g}}$&0\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$ odd&$\scriptstyle{\frac{l}{\gcd(k,l)}\big(\overline{(h_{2}/2)}\overline{g}^{2}+\overline{h_{1}}\overline{g}\big)}$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{l}{\gcd(k,l)}\big(\overline{(h_{2}/2)}\overline{g}^{2}+\overline{h_{1}}\overline{g}\big)}$&$0$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{k}\big(\overline{(h_{2}/2)}\overline{g}^{2}+\overline{h_{1}}\overline{g}\big)}$&0\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}\overline{h_{2}}g(g+1)+\overline{h_{1}}g}$&$\scriptstyle{\overline{(h_{2}/2)}g^{2}+\overline{h}_{1}g}$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}h_{2}g(g+1)+h_{1}g}$&$\scriptstyle{(\overline{h_{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{h}_{1})g^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{h}_{1}g}$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}h_{2}g^{2}+h_{1}g}$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\scriptstyle{h_{1}\overline{g}}$&0\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}h_{2}g^{2}+h_{1}g}$&$\scriptstyle{\frac{1}{2}h_{2}g^{2}+h_{1}g}$\end{tabular}\;.

(118)

To save space we have omitted “
\operatorname{mod}l
” in every entry of the first and second column, and “
\operatorname{mod}1
” in the fourth column.

h/2
denotes the unique element of
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
(
k
odd) such that
h/2+h/2=h
.
It is straightforward but tedious to verify that the above table entries are indeed quadratic functions in the argument
g
, and we do so in Appendix
.
In computations, we will sometimes need to find the bilinear form
h^{(2)}
for a normalized quadratic function
h
on the level of coefficient groups.
The following table lists

\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2}[G,G|A]^{-1}(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}[G|A](h_{2},h_{1})^{(2)})

(119)

for
(h_{2},h_{1})\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[G|A]
,

\begin{tabular}[]{r|c|c|c|c|c}\diagbox[]{{\shortstack[l]{$\scriptstyle{G}$}}}{{\shortstack[r]{$\scriptstyle{A}$}}}&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$ even&$\mathbb{Z}_{l}$ odd&$\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$\mathbb{R}$\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}_{k}$ even&$(\frac{\gcd(k,l)}{\gcd(k,\frac{l}{2})}\overline{h}_{2}-2\overline{h}_{1})\operatorname{mod}\gcd(k,l)$&$h_{2}$&$0$&$(\overline{h}_{2}-2\overline{h}_{1})\operatorname{mod}k$&0\\
\hline\cr[]

\hline\cr$\mathbb{Z}$&$h_{2}$&$h_{2}$&$h_{2}$&$(2\overline{h}_{2}-\overline{h}_{1})\operatorname{mod}1$&$h_{2}$\\
\hline\cr[]
$\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$0$&0\\
\hline\cr$\mathbb{R}$&$0$&$0$&$0$&$h_{2}$&$h_{2}$\end{tabular}\;.

for

Eqs. (

) and (

) can be used to show that the coefficient groups in Eq. (
117
) do contain all normalized quadratic functions.
In Appendix
, we explicitly list a choice of the standard function
Q_{0}(b)
, and we describe
\hom_{2}[G|A]
as a product of
\operatorname{image}(\bullet^{(2)})
and
\hom[G|A]
twisted by a group 2-cocycle
\Omega
.

Putting it all together —

Let us now summarize the results from this and the previous section and spell out explicitly what coefficients are necessary to specify
q
and
\epsilon
of a quadratic tensor data where
G
is a product of
n
elementary abelian groups
G_{i}
and
E
is a product of
m
elementary abelian groups
E_{j}
.

The quadratic function
q\in F_{2}[E|\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}]
is fully specified by

•

q_{a}^{(\tilde{0})}\coloneqq q_{a}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}
and
q_{\phi}^{(\tilde{0})}\coloneqq q_{\phi}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.

•

q^{(\tilde{1})}_{a,i}\coloneqq\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}^{-1}((q_{a})^{(1)}_{i})\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[E_{i}|\mathbb{R}]
and
q^{(\tilde{1})}_{\phi,i}\coloneqq\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}^{-1}((q_{\phi})^{(1)}_{i})\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[E_{i}|\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}]
for every
0\leq i<m
, each of which consists of two elementary abelian coefficients in general.
In practice, we arrange these coefficients into length-
m
vectors which we call
q_{a}^{(\tilde{1})}
and
q_{\phi}^{(\tilde{1})}
.

•

q^{(\tilde{2})}_{a,ij}\coloneqq(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2})^{-1}((q_{a})^{(2)}_{ij})\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}[E_{i},E_{j}|\mathbb{R}]
and
q^{(\tilde{2})}_{\phi,ij}\coloneqq(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2})^{-1}((q_{\phi})^{(2)}_{ij})\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}[E_{i},E_{j}|\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}]
for every
0\leq i<j<m
.
In practice, we arrange these coefficients into strictly upper triangular
m\times m
matrices which we call
q_{a}^{(\tilde{2})}
and
q_{\phi}^{(\tilde{2})}
.
We omit the entries on and below the diagonal, such that the matrix looks like a non-strictly-upper triangular
(m-1)\times(m-1)
matrix.

Note that we can turn the upper triangular matrix
q^{(\tilde{2})}
into a full symmetric matrix.
The diagonal entries
q^{(\tilde{2})}_{ii}
are determined by
q^{(\tilde{1})}_{i}
via Eq. (
120
).

The linear function
\epsilon\in F_{1}[E|G]
is fully specified by

•

\epsilon^{(\tilde{0})}_{i}\coloneqq\epsilon^{(0)}_{i}\in G_{i}
for every
0<i<n
, which we arrange into a length-
n
vector
\epsilon^{(\tilde{0})}
.

•

\epsilon^{(\tilde{1})}_{ij}\coloneqq\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{-1}(\epsilon^{(1)}_{ij})\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[E_{j}|G_{i}]
, which we arrange into an
n\times m
matrix
\epsilon^{(\tilde{1})}
.

III.5
Homomorphism composition

In this section, we show how to compute the composition of a quadratic function with a homomorphism in terms of the underlying coefficients.
This will be necessary for computing reductions as in Section
III.6
.

{myprop}

Consider a quadratic function
q\in F_{2}[G|A]
, and a homomorphism
\gamma\in\hom[H|G]
.
The precomposition of
q
with
\gamma
,

q\circ\gamma:H\rightarrow A\;,\quad(q\circ\gamma)(h)\coloneqq q(\gamma(h))\;,

(123)

is again a quadratic function,
q\circ\gamma\in F_{2}[H|A]
.

Proof.

We compute

\begin{gathered}(q\circ\gamma)^{(2)}(h,h^{\prime})=q(\gamma(h+h^{\prime}))-q(\gamma(h))-q(\gamma(h^{\prime}))+q(0)=q(\gamma(h)+\gamma(h^{\prime}))-q(\gamma(h))-q(\gamma(h^{\prime}))+q(0)\\
=q^{(2)}(\gamma(h),\gamma(h^{\prime}))=(q^{(2)}\circ(\gamma\times\gamma))(h,h^{\prime})\;.\end{gathered}

(124)

So
q\circ\gamma
is indeed a quadratic function with associated bilinear form
q^{(2)}\circ(\gamma\times\gamma)
.
∎

Let us now assume that
H
and
G
are products of many factors, and
q
and
\gamma
are given in terms of their components as in Section
III.3
.
We note that
\gamma
is a homomorphism, which is a linear function with
\gamma^{(0)}=0
, so
\gamma
is specified by the components
\gamma^{(1)}_{ij}
alone, which we simply denote by
\gamma_{ij}
.

{myprop}

The components of
q\circ\gamma
are given by

\begin{gathered}(q\circ\gamma)^{(2)}_{ij}=\sum_{k,l}q^{(2)}_{kl}\circ(\gamma_{ki}\times\gamma_{lj})\;,\\
(q\circ\gamma)^{(1)}_{i}(h)=\sum_{k}(q^{(1)}_{k}\circ\gamma_{ki})(h)+\sum_{l<k}q^{(2)}_{kl}\circ(\gamma_{ki}\times\gamma_{li})(h,h)\;,\\
(q\circ\gamma)^{(0)}=q^{(0)}\;.\end{gathered}

(125)

Proof.

For the first equation, we find

\begin{gathered}(q\circ\gamma)^{(2)}_{ij}(h,h^{\prime})=(q\circ\gamma)^{(2)}(h|_{i},h^{\prime}|_{j})=q^{(2)}(\gamma(h|_{i}),\gamma(h^{\prime}|_{j}))\\
=\sum_{k,l}q^{(2)}(\gamma_{ki}(h)|_{k},\gamma_{lj}(h^{\prime})|_{l})=\sum_{k,l}q^{(2)}_{kl}\circ(\gamma_{ki}\times\gamma_{lj})(h,h^{\prime})\;.\end{gathered}

(126)

For the second equation, we find

\begin{gathered}(q\circ\gamma)^{(1)}_{i}(h)=(q\circ\gamma)^{(1)}(h|_{i})=q^{(1)}(\gamma(h|_{i}))=q^{(1)}(\sum_{k}\gamma_{ki}(h)|_{k})\\
=\sum_{k}q^{(1)}(\gamma_{ki}(h)|_{k})+\sum_{l<k}q^{(2)}(\gamma_{ki}(h)|_{k},\gamma_{li}(h)|_{l})=\sum_{k}q^{(1)}_{k}(\gamma_{ki}(h))+\sum_{l<k}q^{(2)}_{kl}(\gamma_{ki}(h),\gamma_{li}(h))\;.\end{gathered}

(127)

The third equation is trivial,

(q\circ\gamma)^{(0)}=(q\circ\gamma)(0)=q(\gamma(0))=q(0)=q^{(0)}\;.

(128)

∎

Finally, let us assume that all the factors of
H
and
G
are elementary abelian groups, and that all the components of
q
and
\gamma
are given as explicit coefficients as described in Section
III.4
.
Note that we will write
\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}
instead of
\gamma^{(\tilde{1})}_{ij}
, as
\gamma^{(\tilde{0})}_{ij}
is trivial.
We will need the following notion of a dual.

{mydef}

For each abelian group
G
, define the
dual
with respect to an abelian group
A
as
8
8

8

If
A=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, this is known as the
Pontryagin dual
and we have
G^{**}\simeq G
.
For general
A
, we may have
G^{**}\not\simeq G
, so the name “dual” is maybe not entirely justified.
Instead,
G
can be identified with a subgroup of
G^{**}
,
G\subset G^{**}
via the injective homomorphism

\operatorname{ev}\in\hom[G|G^{**}]:\qquad(\operatorname{ev}(g))(g^{\prime})=g^{\prime}(g)\quad\forall g^{\prime}\in G^{*},\quad g\in G\;.

G^{*}\coloneqq\hom[G|A]\;.

(129)

For each homomorphism
\gamma\in\hom[H|G]
, define the
dual
as

\gamma^{*}\in\hom[G^{*}|H^{*}]:\qquad\gamma^{*}(g^{\prime})(h)=g^{\prime}(\gamma(h))\quad\forall g^{\prime}\in G^{*},\quad h\in H\;.

(130)

{myprop}

Let
H
and
G
be products of elementary abelian groups,
A
an elementary abelian group,
\gamma\in\hom[H|G]
, and
q\in F_{2}[G|A]
.
Then the coefficients of
q\circ\gamma
can be calculated from these of
q
as follows

\begin{gathered}(q\circ\gamma)^{(\tilde{2})}_{ij}=\sum_{k,l}\tilde{\gamma}_{ki}^{\tilde{*}}\;\tilde{\circ}\;q^{(\tilde{2})}_{kl}\;\tilde{\circ}\;\tilde{\gamma}_{lj}\;,\\
(q\circ\gamma)^{(\tilde{1})}_{i}=\sum_{k}\Phi(q^{(\tilde{1})}_{k},\tilde{\gamma}_{ki})+\sum_{l<k}\Lambda(\tilde{\gamma}_{ki}^{\tilde{*}}\;\tilde{\circ}\;q^{(\tilde{2})}_{kl}\;\tilde{\circ}\;\tilde{\gamma}_{li})\;,\\
(q\circ\gamma)^{(\tilde{0})}=q^{(\tilde{0})}\;.\end{gathered}

(131)

Here,
h^{\prime}\;\tilde{\circ}\;h
for
h\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{0}|G_{1}]
and
h^{\prime}\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{1}|G_{2}]
for elementary abelian groups
G_{0}
,
G_{1}
, and
G_{2}
denotes the composition of homomorphisms conjugated by the coefficient isomorphism
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H
,

h\;\tilde{\circ}\;h^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[G_{0}|G_{2}]^{-1}\big(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[G_{1}|G_{2}](h^{\prime})\circ\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[G_{0}|G_{1}](h)\big)\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{0}|G_{2}]\;.

(132)

It is listed for different
G_{0}
,
G_{1}
, and
G_{2}
below in Eq. (
138
).
In order to apply this, we note that by definition in Eq. (
109
), we have

q^{(\tilde{2})}_{kl}\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}[G_{k},G_{l}|A]=\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{k}|\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{l}|A]]\;.

(133)

\gamma^{\tilde{*}}
for
\gamma\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[H_{0}|G_{0}]
denotes the dual of a homomorphism conjugated with the coefficient isomorphism
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H
,

\gamma^{\tilde{*}}=\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{-1}[\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[G_{0}|A]|\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}[H_{0}|A]]\big(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[H_{0}|G_{0}](\gamma)^{*}\big)\;.

(134)

It is listed below in Eq. (
139
).

\Phi(q,\gamma)
for
q\in\hom_{2}[G_{0}|A]
and
\gamma\in\hom[H_{0}|G_{0}]
and elementary groups
G_{0}
,
H_{0}
and
A
is given by the composition of
q
with
\gamma
, conjugated by the coefficient isomorphisms
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H
and
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}
,

\Phi[G_{0},H_{0}|A](q,\gamma)\coloneqq\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}[H_{0}|A]^{-1}\big(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}[G_{0}|A](q)\circ\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H[H_{0}|G_{0}](\gamma)\big)\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}_{2}[H_{0}|A]\;.

(135)

It is listed for different
G_{0}
,
H_{0}
, and
A
in Eqs. (
140
) and (
141
).
Last, we can turn a bilinear form
b\in\hom^{2}[G_{0},G_{0}|A]
into a quadratic function
q\in\hom_{2}[G_{0}|A]
by taking twice the same argument,
q(g)=b(g,g)=b\circ\operatorname{Copy}(g)
or
q=b\circ\operatorname{Copy}
.

\Lambda(h)
for
h\in\mathrlap{\raisebox{-3.44444pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{\operatorname{hom}}}$}}\operatorname{hom}^{2}[G_{0},G_{0}|A]
denotes that same operation conjugated by the coefficient isomorphisms,

\Lambda[G_{0}|A](h)\coloneqq\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}[G_{0}|A]^{-1}\big(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2}[G_{0},G_{0}|A](h)\circ\operatorname{Copy}\big)\;.

(136)

\Lambda
is listed for different
G_{0}
and
A
in Eq. (
142
).

Proof.

This follows immediately from conjugating both sides of Eq. (

) by the coefficient isomorphisms
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H
,
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2}
, and
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H_{2}
.
Thereby, we note that we can rewrite

q^{(2)}_{kl}\circ(\gamma_{ki}\times\gamma_{lj})=\gamma_{lj}^{*}\circ q^{(2)}_{kl}\circ\gamma_{ki}\;,

(137)

using the identification in Eq. (

).
∎

We now give the explicit formulas for
\;\tilde{\circ}\;

.

:

(139)

) for different elementary abelian groups

.

:

.

III.6
Contraction and reduction

In this section, we discuss how to contract tensor networks of quadratic tensors directly in terms of the underlying coefficients
E
,
\epsilon^{(\tilde{1})}
,
\epsilon^{(\tilde{0})}
,
q^{(\tilde{2})}
,
q^{(\tilde{1})}
, and
q^{(\tilde{0})}
.

Tensor product and self-contraction —

We start by showing that tensor networks of quadratic tensors result again in quadratic tensors.

{myprop}

Consider a quadratic tensor data
(E,\epsilon,q)
over
G
, and another quadratic tensor data
(E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime})
over
G^{\prime}
.
Then the tensor product of the associated quadratic tensors is again a quadratic tensor, with data given given as follows:

\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q]\otimes\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime}]=\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime\prime}]\;,\qquad E^{\prime\prime}=E\times E^{\prime},\quad\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=\epsilon\times\epsilon^{\prime},\quad q^{\prime\prime}((e,e^{\prime}))=q(e)+q^{\prime}(e^{\prime})\;.

(143)

If one of the quadratic tensor data is
0
, the tensor product is
0
.

Proof.

\begin{gathered}(\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q]\otimes\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime}])(g,g^{\prime})=\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q](g)\cdot\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime}](g^{\prime})\\
=\sum_{e\in E:\epsilon(e)=g}\exp(q(e))\cdot\sum_{e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}:\epsilon^{\prime}(e^{\prime})=g^{\prime}}\exp(q^{\prime}(e^{\prime}))=\sum_{(e,e^{\prime})\in E\times E^{\prime}:(\epsilon\times\epsilon^{\prime})(e,e^{\prime})=(g,g^{\prime})}\exp(q(e)+q^{\prime}(e^{\prime}))\\
=\sum_{e\in E^{\prime\prime}:\epsilon^{\prime\prime}(e)=(g,g^{\prime})}\exp(q^{\prime\prime}(e))=\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime},q^{\prime\prime}](g,g^{\prime})\;.\end{gathered}

(144)

∎

{myprop}

Let
G_{r}
and
G_{c}
be abelian groups.
Consider a quadratic tensor data
(E,\epsilon,q)
over
G=G_{r}\times G_{c}\times G_{c}
, and write
\epsilon=\epsilon_{0}\times\epsilon_{1}\times\epsilon_{2}
.
Then, the self-contraction of the two
G_{c}
indices is again a quadratic tensor over
G_{r}
with data given by

\begin{gathered}\sum_{g_{c}\in G_{c}}\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q](g_{r},g_{c},g_{c})=\begin{cases}\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime}](g_{r})&\text{if }\epsilon_{2}^{(0)}-\epsilon_{1}^{(0)}\in\operatorname{image}(\epsilon_{1}^{(1)}-\epsilon_{2}^{(1)})\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\\
E^{\prime}=\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon_{1}^{(1)}-\epsilon_{2}^{(1)})\;,\quad\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon_{0}\circ(\kappa+\tilde{e})\;,\quad q^{\prime}=q\circ(\kappa+\tilde{e})\;.\end{gathered}

(145)

Here,
\kappa\in\hom[E^{\prime}|E]
is the homomorphism that identifies
E^{\prime}
as a subgroup of
E
, and
\tilde{e}\in E
is chosen such that
(\epsilon_{1}^{(1)}-\epsilon_{2}^{(1)})\tilde{e}=\epsilon_{2}^{(0)}-\epsilon_{1}^{(0)}
, or equivalently
\epsilon_{1}(\tilde{e})=\epsilon_{2}(\tilde{e})
.

Proof.

The second case in Eq. (
145
) is obvious.
For the first case, we find

\begin{gathered}\sum_{g_{c}\in G_{c}}\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q](g_{r},g_{c},g_{c})=\sum_{g_{c}\in G_{c},e\in E:\epsilon_{0}(e)=g_{r},\epsilon_{1}(e)=g_{c},\epsilon_{2}(e)=g_{c}}\exp(q(e))=\sum_{e\in E:\epsilon_{0}(e)=g_{r},\epsilon_{1}(e)=\epsilon_{2}(e)}\exp(q(e))\\
=\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}:\epsilon_{0}(\kappa e+\tilde{e})=g_{r}}\exp(q(\kappa e+\tilde{e}))=\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}:\epsilon^{\prime}(e)=g_{r}}\exp(q^{\prime}(e))=\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime}](g_{r})\;.\end{gathered}

(146)

∎

An arbitrary tensor network can be contracted by first performing pairwise tensor products until there is a single tensor, and then performing self-contractions of all the index pairs.
So we see that it is in principle possible to evaluate arbitrary tensor networks of quadratic tensors in terms of the underlying quadratic tensor data.
However, this evaluation leads to a growing group
E
.
Roughly speaking, if we evaluate a tensor network with
n

G_{c}
contractions, then the resulting
E
is by up to
n

G_{c}
factors larger than the resulting
G
.
So we merely shifted the computational cost from the contraction to the calculation of the resulting tensor entries
\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q]
in Eq. (
80
).
To implement contraction in a practically useful way, we thus also need a way to reduce the size of
E
, which we do in the following.

Reduction via invertible subgroups —

We will now show two ways to
reduce
a quadratic tensor data
(E,\epsilon,q)
to an equivalent quadratic tensor data
(E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime})
, such that
E^{\prime}
is smaller than
E
, in the sense that it consists of fewer elementary factors.
Roughly, the first type of reduction can be applied if the bilinear form
q^{(2)}
is non-degenerate on some subgroup of
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon)
, and the second reduction can be applied if
q^{(2)}
is zero on some subgroup of
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon)
.
The following two propositions are two variants of the first type of reduction.
We will use the notation for duals from Definition
III.5
, and use the isomorphism from Eq. (
108
) implicitly in the notation.
We denote composition of homomorphisms by just writing them in sequence, saving space by omitting the usual
\circ
symbol.
Combining the above notations, we can for example write

q^{(2)}(\gamma(h),g)=q^{(2)}\gamma(h,g)\;,\qquad q^{(2)}(g,\gamma h)=\gamma^{*}q^{(2)}(g,h)

(147)

for
h\in H
,
g\in G
,
q^{(2)}\in\hom^{2}[G,G|A]\simeq\hom[G|G^{*}]
,
\gamma\in\hom[H|G]
.

For reasons that will come clear later, we will consider the notion of quadratic tensors for some general abelian
A
, instead of the more specific physics use case
A=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.

{myprop}

Let
(E,\epsilon,q)
be a quadratic tensor data over
G
.
Let
\rho\in\hom[R|E]
be an injective homomorphism that identifies
R
as a subgroup of
E
, such that the following holds.

•

E
is a subgroup of the kernel of
\epsilon^{(1)}
,

\epsilon^{(1)}\rho=0\;.

(148)

•

q^{(2)}
is non-degenerate on
R
, that is,

\rho^{*}q^{(2)}\rho\in\hom[R|R^{*}]

(149)

has an inverse

q^{\text{inv}}\in\hom[R^{*}|R]:\qquad\rho^{*}q^{(2)}\rho q^{\text{inv}}=1_{R^{*}},\quad q^{\text{inv}}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}\rho=1_{R}\;.

(150)

•

The exponential sum of
q
over
R
is itself an exponential (and in particular non-zero):

\sum_{r\in R}\exp(q(\rho(r)))=\exp(q^{\sum}),\quad q^{\sum}\in A\;.

(151)

Then the following quadratic tensor data
(E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is equivalent to
(E,\epsilon,q)
:

\displaystyle E^{\prime}

\displaystyle=E/R\;,

(152)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}(r^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))\;,

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(r^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{q}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})),\quad\widetilde{q}(e)\coloneqq q(e)-q(\gamma(e))+q^{\sum}\;,

where

\gamma\coloneqq\rho q^{\text{inv}}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}\in\hom[E|E]\;,

(153)

and

\rho^{\perp}:E/R\rightarrow E:\qquad(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))/R=r^{\perp}

(154)

is any left inverse of the quotient map from
E
to
E/R
.

Proof.

Note that
\widetilde{q}\in F_{2}(E,A)
is a quadratic function since it is obtained from
q
via sums, homomorphism composition (see Proposition
III.5
), and constants.
Further, we find that
\widetilde{q}
is invariant under adding elements of
R
to its argument:

\begin{gathered}\widetilde{q}(e+\rho(r))\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered}}{=}q(e+\rho(r))-q(\gamma(e+\rho(r)))+q^{\sum}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation6}}{=}q(e+\rho(r))-q(\gamma(e)+\rho(r)))+q^{\sum}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}q(e)+q(\rho(r))+q^{(2)}(\rho(r),e)-q(\gamma(e))-q(\rho(r))-q^{(2)}(\rho(r),\gamma(e))+q^{\sum}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation7}}{=}q(e)-q(\gamma(e))+q^{\sum}=\widetilde{q}(e)\;.\end{gathered}

(155)

Here, we have used that

\displaystyle\gamma\rho=\rho\;,\quad q^{(2)}\gamma=\gamma^{*}q^{(2)}\;,

(156)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow q^{(2)}(\rho(r),e)=q^{(2)}\rho(r,e)=q^{(2)}\gamma\rho(r,e)=\gamma^{*}q^{(2)}\rho(r,e)=q^{(2)}(\rho(r),\gamma(e))\;.

(157)

Thus, we can reduce
\widetilde{q}
to a quadratic function
q^{\prime}
on
E/R
, which is independent of the choice of
\rho^{\perp}
.
Let us now confirm Eq. (
81
).
First, we find that

\begin{gathered}\sum_{r\in R}\exp(q(e+\rho(r)))\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}\sum_{r\in R}\exp\big(q(e)+q(\rho(r))+q^{(2)}(\rho(r),e)-q(0)\big)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation7}}{=}\sum_{r\in R}\exp\big(q(e)+q(\rho(r))+q^{(2)}(\rho(r),\gamma(e))-q(0)\big)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}\sum_{r\in R}\exp\big(q(e)+q\big(\rho(r)+\gamma(e)\big)-q(\gamma(e))\big)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_gamma_def}}{=}\exp\big(q(e)-q(\gamma(e))\big)\sum_{r\in R}\exp\big(q(\rho(r+q^{\text{inv}}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(e)))\big)\\
\overset{*}{=}\exp\big(q(e)-q(\gamma(e))\big)\sum_{r\in R}\exp(q(\rho(r)))\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation5}}{=}\exp\big(q(e)-q(\gamma(e))+q^{\sum}\big)\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered}}{=}\exp(\widetilde{q}(e))\;.\end{gathered}

(158)

For the equality labeled
*
, we have performed a change in summation variable
r\rightarrow r-q^{\text{inv}}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(e)
.
Thus, we have

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{T}[E,e,q](g)\overset{\eqref{eq:quadratic_tensor_definition}}{=}\sum_{e\in E:\epsilon(e)=g}\exp(q(e))\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation1},\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation2}}{=}\sum_{r\in R,r^{\perp}\in E/R:\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=g}\exp(q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})+\rho(r)))\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation3}}{=}\sum_{r^{\perp}\in E/R:\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=g}\exp(\widetilde{q}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})))\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered}}{=}\sum_{r^{\perp}\in E^{\prime}:\epsilon^{\prime}(r^{\perp})=g}\exp(q^{\prime}(r^{\perp}))\overset{\eqref{eq:quadratic_tensor_definition}}{=}\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime}](g)\;.\end{gathered}

(159)

Here, we have used that for every
e\in E
, there are unique
r\in R
and
r^{\perp}\in E/R
, such that

e=\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})+\rho(r)\;.

(160)

∎

The reduction above holds for any choice of
A
and
\rho
.
However, if
A=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, then we can never find a non-trivial subgroup such that Eq. (
150
) holds.
So we can only really apply the reduction above if
A=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
, which is equivalent to saying that
q_{a}=0
.
In fact, it suffices if
q_{a}
is zero restricted to the subgroup
R
, which is automatic if
R
only consists of
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
or
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
factors.

In Section
II
, we have seen that we can also perform reduction over (continuous-variable)
\mathbb{R}^{n}
subgroups, where both
q_{\phi}
and
q_{a}
are non-zero.
In this contraction, we treat
q_{a}^{(2)}+iq_{\phi}^{(2)}
as a complex number, and we perform the inverse
q_{11}^{-1}
in the field of complex numbers.
If we want to define such a contraction in a more general case where only the subgroup
R
is equal to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, but the full group
E
can be arbitrary, we have to keep real and imaginary parts such as
q_{a}
and
q_{\phi}
separate.
The result is the following, slightly lengthy reduction.

In the following, we use the notation
X^{*}=\hom[X|\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}]
, and
X^{+}=\hom[X|\mathbb{R}]
for any abelian group
X
, and the analogous notation
\rho^{*}\in\hom[Y^{*}|X^{*}]
and
\rho^{+}\in\hom[Y^{+}|X^{+}]
for a homomorphism
\rho\in\hom[X|Y]
.
We also make use of an isomorphism

\chi:(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{+}\rightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}\;:\qquad\chi(x)(\vec{r})=x(\vec{r})\operatorname{mod}1\;.

(161)

{myprop}

Let
(E,\epsilon,q)
be a quadratic tensor data over
G
.
Let
\rho\in\hom[\mathbb{R}^{n}|E]
be an injective homomorphism that identifies
\mathbb{R}^{n}
as a subgroup of
E
, such that the following holds.

•

Eq. (
148
) holds.

•

There exist

q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\in\hom[(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{+}|\mathbb{R}^{n}]\;,\qquad q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}\in\hom[(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}|\mathbb{R}^{n}]

(162)

such that

\begin{gathered}\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}\rho q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\chi^{-1}-\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}\rho q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}=1,\qquad\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}\rho q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}+\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}\rho q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\chi^{-1}=0\;,\\
(q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}-q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)})\rho=1,\qquad(q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}+q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)})\rho=0\;.\end{gathered}

(163)

•

q
is integrable over the
\mathbb{R}^{n}
subgroup,

\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(\rho(r))),\quad q_{\phi}(\rho(r))\Big)=\exp(q_{a}^{\sum},q_{\phi}^{\sum})\;.

(164)

Define
\beta_{a},\beta_{\phi}\in\hom[E|\mathbb{R}^{n}]
, and
\gamma_{a},\gamma_{\phi}\in\hom[E|E]
as

\begin{gathered}\beta_{a}\coloneqq q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}-q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}\;,\quad\beta_{\phi}\coloneqq q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}+q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}\;,\quad\gamma_{a}\coloneqq\rho\beta_{a}\;,\quad\gamma_{\phi}\coloneqq\rho\beta_{\phi}\;.\end{gathered}

(165)

Then the following quadratic tensor data
(E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime})
is equivalent to
(E,\epsilon,q)
:

\displaystyle E^{\prime}

\displaystyle=E/R\;,

(166)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}(r^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))\;,

\displaystyle q_{a}^{\prime}(r^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{q}_{a}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})),\quad\widetilde{q}_{a}(e)=q_{a}(e)-q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e))+q_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e))+q_{a}^{\sum}

\displaystyle q_{\phi}^{\prime}(r^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{q}_{\phi}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})),\quad\widetilde{q}_{\phi}(e)=q_{\phi}(e)-q_{\phi}(\gamma_{a}(e))+q_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))-\chi\rho^{+}q^{(2)}_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e))+q_{\phi}^{\sum}\;.

Proof.

As a first step, we show that
\widetilde{q}_{a}
and
\widetilde{q}_{\phi}
are independent of choice of
\rho^{\perp}
, analogous to Eq. (
155
):

\begin{gathered}\widetilde{q}_{a}(e+\rho(r))\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex}}{=}q_{a}(e+\rho(r))-q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e+\rho(r)))+q_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e+\rho(r)))+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e+\rho(r)),\beta_{a}(e+\rho(r)))+q_{a}^{\sum}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex_derivation0},\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex_derivation1}}{=}q_{a}(e+\rho(r))-q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e)+\rho(r))+q_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r)+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e))+q_{a}^{\sum}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}q_{a}(e)+q_{a}(\rho(r))+q_{a}^{(2)}(e,\rho(r))-q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e))-q_{a}(\rho(r))-q^{(2)}_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e),\rho(r))\\
+q_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r)+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e))+q_{a}^{\sum}\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex_derivation2}}{=}q_{a}(e)-q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e))+q_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e))+q_{a}^{\sum}=\widetilde{q}_{a}(e)\;.\end{gathered}

(167)

Here, we have used that

\displaystyle\beta_{a}\rho\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex_derivation3}}{=}1\;,

(168)

\displaystyle\beta_{\phi}\rho\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex_derivation3}}{=}0\;,

(169)

\displaystyle\begin{multlined}\rho^{+}q^{(2)}_{a}\gamma_{a}-\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}\gamma_{\phi}=\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}\rho(q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}-q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)})-\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}\rho(q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}+q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)})\\
=(\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}\rho q^{\text{inv}}_{a}-\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}\rho q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}\chi)\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}-(\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}\rho q^{\text{inv}}_{\phi}+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}\rho q^{\text{inv}}_{a}\chi^{-1})\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}=\rho^{+}q^{(2)}_{a}\;,\\
\Rightarrow q_{a}^{(2)}(e,\rho(r))=q_{a}^{(2)}(\gamma_{a}(e),\rho(r))-\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r)\;,\end{multlined}

\displaystyle\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}\gamma_{a}+\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}\gamma_{\phi}=\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}\quad\Rightarrow\quad q_{\phi}^{(2)}(e,\rho(r))=q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\gamma_{a}(e),\rho(r))+\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r)\;.

(173)

We omit the same computation for
\widetilde{q}_{\phi}
as it is very similar.
Analogous to Eq. (
158
) we find:

\begin{gathered}\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(e+\rho(r)),\quad q_{\phi}(e+\rho(r))\Big)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(e)+q_{a}(\rho(r))+q_{a}^{(2)}(e,\rho(r))-q_{a}(0),\quad q_{\phi}(e)+q_{\phi}(\rho(r))+q_{\phi}^{(2)}(e,\rho(r))-q_{\phi}(0)\Big)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex_derivation2},\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex_derivation4}}{=}\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(e)+q_{a}(\rho(r))+q_{a}^{(2)}(\gamma_{a}(e),\rho(r))-\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r)-q_{a}(0),\\
q_{\phi}(e)+q_{\phi}(\rho(r))+q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\gamma_{a}(e),\rho(r))+\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r)-q_{\phi}(0)\Big)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(e)+q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e)+\rho(r))-q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e))-\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r),\\
q_{\phi}(e)+q_{\phi}(\gamma_{a}(e)+\rho(r))-q_{\phi}(\gamma_{a}(e))+\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r)\Big)\\
\begin{multlined}=\exp\Big(q_{a}(e)-q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e))+q_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e)),\\
q_{\phi}(e)-q_{\phi}(\gamma_{a}(e))+q_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))-\chi\rho^{+}q^{(2)}_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e))\Big)\\
\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(\rho(r+\beta_{a}(e)))-q_{a}(\rho(\beta_{\phi}(e)))-\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r+\beta_{a}(e)),\\
q_{\phi}(\rho(r+\beta_{a}(e)))-q_{\phi}(\rho(\beta_{\phi}(e)))+\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),r+\beta_{a}(e))\Big)\end{multlined}\\
\overset{*}{=}\exp\Big(q_{a}(e)-q_{a}(\gamma_{a}(e))+q_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))+\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e)),\\
q_{\phi}(e)-q_{\phi}(\gamma_{a}(e))+q_{\phi}(\gamma_{\phi}(e))-\chi\rho^{+}q^{(2)}_{a}(\gamma_{\phi}(e),\beta_{a}(e))\Big)\\
\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(\rho(r))),\quad q_{\phi}(\rho(r))\Big)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_complex}}{=}\exp\Big(\tilde{q}_{a}(e),\quad\tilde{q}_{\phi}(e)\Big)\;.\end{gathered}

(174)

For the equality labeled
*
, we have used that
q_{a}(\rho(\bullet))
and
q_{\phi}(\rho(\bullet))
are quadratic functions
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and take the following simple form,

q_{a}(\rho(r))=\frac{1}{2}r^{T}A_{a}r+d_{a}r+c_{a}\;,\quad q_{\phi}(\rho(r))=\frac{1}{2}r^{T}A_{\phi}r+d_{\phi}r+c_{\phi}\mod 1\;.

(175)

Using this concrete form, we can calculate the integral,

\begin{gathered}\begin{multlined}\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(\rho(r+\beta_{a}(e)))-q_{a}(\rho(\beta_{\phi}(e)))-\chi^{-1}\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}(\rho(r+\beta_{a}(e)),\beta_{\phi}(e)),\\
q_{\phi}(\rho(r+\beta_{a}(e)))-q_{\phi}(\rho(\beta_{\phi}(e)))+\chi\rho^{+}q_{a}^{(2)}(\rho(r+\beta_{a}(e)),\beta_{\phi}(e))\Big)\end{multlined}\\
\begin{multlined}=\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}(r+\beta_{a}(e))^{T}A_{a}(r+\beta_{a}(e))+d_{a}(r+\beta_{a}(e))+c_{a}\\
-\beta_{\phi}(e)^{T}A_{a}\beta_{\phi}(e)-d_{a}\beta_{\phi}(e)-c_{a}-(r+\beta_{a}(e))^{T}A_{\phi}\beta_{\phi}(e),\\
\frac{1}{2}(r+\beta_{a}(e))^{T}A_{\phi}(r+\beta_{a}(e))+d_{\phi}(r+\beta_{a}(e))+c_{\phi}\\
-\beta_{\phi}(e)^{T}A_{\phi}\beta_{\phi}(e)-d_{\phi}\beta_{\phi}(e)-c_{\phi}-(r+\beta_{a}(e))^{T}A_{a}\beta_{\phi}(e)\Big)\end{multlined}\\
=\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{2\pi\Big(\frac{1}{2}(r+\beta_{a}(e)+i\beta_{\phi}(e))^{T}(A_{a}+iA_{\phi})(r+\beta_{a}(e)+i\beta_{\phi}(e))+(d_{a}+id_{\phi})(r+\beta_{a}(e)+i\beta_{\phi}(e))+c_{a}+c_{\phi}\big)}\\
=\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}e^{2\pi\Big(\frac{1}{2}r^{T}(A_{a}+iA_{\phi})r+(d_{a}+id_{\phi})r+c_{a}+c_{\phi}\big)}\\
\int_{r\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\exp\Big(q_{a}(\rho(r))),\quad q_{\phi}(\rho(r))\Big)\;.\end{gathered}

(176)

Here, we have used that
\exp
is an analytic function that vanishes at infinity, and so we can perform a change of variable
r\rightarrow r+\beta_{a}(e)+i\beta_{\phi}(e)
shifting the real variable
r
by a complex value
\beta_{e}(e)+i\beta_{\phi}(e)
.
With this, the last step of the proof, showing that
(E,\epsilon,q)
and
(E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime})
are equivalent, is the same as in Eq. (
159
).
∎

The reductions above only apply if the quadratic form
q^{(2)}
is invertible on the subgroup
R
.
The second kind of reduction, introduced below, applies in the opposite case, namely when
q^{(2)}
is zero on
R
.

{myprop}

Let
(E,\epsilon,q)
be a quadratic tensor data over a group
G
.
Let
\rho:R\rightarrow E
be an injective homomorphism that identifies
R
as a subgroup of
E
, such that the following holds.

•

Eq. (
148
) holds.

•

The bilinear form
q^{(2)}
is zero on
R
,

\rho^{*}q^{(2)}\rho=0\;.

(177)

Then the quadratic tensor data
(E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime})
defined in the following is equivalent to
(E,\epsilon,q)
.
Let
\rho^{\perp}:E/R\rightarrow E
be a left inverse of the quotient map as in Eq. (
154
).
Consider the homomorphism

\alpha:E/R\rightarrow R^{*}:\qquad\alpha(r^{\perp})=\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))\;.

(178)

If there exists
r^{\perp}_{0}\in E/R
such that

\alpha(r^{\perp}_{0})+q^{(1)}\circ\rho=0\;,

(179)

then define

\displaystyle E^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{kernel}(\alpha)\subset E/R\;,

(180)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}(r^{\perp})

\displaystyle=\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r^{\perp}_{0}))\;,

\displaystyle q^{\prime}(r^{\perp})

\displaystyle=q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r^{\perp}_{0}))+\log(|R|)\;.

Otherwise,
(E,\epsilon,q)
is equivalent to
0
.
Here,
\log
denotes the inverse of
\exp
(which has an additional
2\pi
compared to the usual logarithm).

|R|
denotes the order of the finite-group part of
R
, ignoring factors of
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{Z}
, or
\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let us start by confirming that
E^{\prime}
,
\epsilon^{\prime}
and
q^{\prime}
are well defined.
First, we note that in Eq. (
179
), the composition of functions
q^{(1)}\circ\rho
is an element of
R^{*}
:

(q^{(1)}\circ\rho)(r_{0}+r_{1})\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}q^{(1)}(\rho(r_{0}))+q^{(1)}(\rho(r_{1}))+\rho^{*}q^{(2)}\rho(r_{0},r_{1})\overset{\eqref{eq:zeroreduction_derivation1}}{=}(q^{(1)}\circ\rho)(r_{0})+(q^{(1)}\circ\rho)(r_{1})\;.

(181)

We also need to check that
\alpha
itself defines a valid homomorphism:

\begin{gathered}\alpha(r^{\perp}_{0}+r^{\perp}_{1})\overset{\eqref{eq:zeroreduction_alphadef}}{=}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}_{0}+r^{\perp}_{1}))=\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}_{0})+\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}_{1})+\rho(r))\\
=\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}_{0}))+\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}_{1}))+\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho(r))\overset{\eqref{eq:zeroreduction_derivation1}}{=}\alpha(r^{\perp}_{0})+\alpha(r^{\perp}_{1})\;.\end{gathered}

(182)

Next, we show that
\alpha
(and thus
E^{\prime}
),
\epsilon^{\prime}
, and
q^{\prime}
do not depend on the choice of
\rho^{\perp}
.
That is, they do not change if we replace
\widetilde{\rho}^{\perp}(r^{\perp})\coloneqq\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})+\rho(r)
for some fixed
r\in R
:

\widetilde{\alpha}(r^{\perp})=\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\widetilde{\rho}^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})+\rho(r))\overset{\eqref{eq:zeroreduction_derivation1}}{=}\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=\alpha(r^{\perp})\;,

(183)

\widetilde{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r^{\perp})=\epsilon(\widetilde{\rho}^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp}))=\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp})+\rho(r))=\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp}))+\epsilon^{(1)}\rho(r)\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation2}}{=}\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp}))=\epsilon^{\prime}(r^{\perp})\;,

(184)

\begin{gathered}\widetilde{q}^{\prime}(r^{\perp})=q(\widetilde{\rho}^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp}))+\log(|R|)=q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp})+\rho(r))+\log(|R|)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp}))+q(\rho(r))-q(0)+q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp}),\rho(r))+\log(|R|)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:zeroreduction},\eqref{eq:first_derivative}}{=}q^{\prime}(r^{\perp})+(q^{(1)}\circ\rho)(r)+\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp}),r)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:zeroreduction_alphadef}}{=}q^{\prime}(r^{\perp})+(q^{(1)}\circ\rho)(r)+\alpha(r^{\perp}_{0})(r)+\alpha(r^{\perp})(r)\overset{*,\eqref{eq:zeroreduction_offset}}{=}q^{\prime}(r^{\perp})\;.\end{gathered}

(185)

For the equality labeled
*
, we have used that by assumption
r^{\perp}\in\operatorname{kernel}(\alpha)
.
Finally, we confirm Eq. (
81
) by direct calculation:

\begin{gathered}\mathcal{T}[E,\epsilon,q](g)\overset{\eqref{eq:quadratic_tensor_definition}}{=}\sum_{e\in E:\epsilon(e)=g}\exp(q(e))\overset{\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation1},\eqref{eq:invertiblered_derivation2}}{=}\sum_{r^{\perp}\in E/R:\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=g}\sum_{r\in R}\exp(q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})+\rho(r)))\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:second_derivative}}{=}\sum_{r^{\perp}\in E/R:\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=g}\sum_{r\in R}\exp\big(q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))+q(\rho(r))+q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}),\rho(r))-q(0)\big)\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:first_derivative}}{=}\sum_{r^{\perp}\in E/R:\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=g}\exp(q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})))\sum_{r\in R}\exp(q^{(1)}(\rho(r))+\rho^{*}q^{(2)}(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}),r))\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:zeroreduction_alphadef}}{=}\sum_{r^{\perp}\in E/R:\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=g}\exp(q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})))\sum_{r\in R}\exp((\alpha(r^{\perp})+q^{(1)}\circ\rho)(r))\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:group_character_sum}}{=}\sum_{r^{\perp}\in E/R:\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=g}\exp(q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp})))|R|\delta_{\alpha(r^{\perp})+q^{(1)}\circ\rho=0}\\
=\sum_{r^{\perp}\in E/R:\alpha(r^{\perp})+q^{(1)}\circ\rho=0,\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))=g}\exp(q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}))+\log(|R|))\\
\overset{*,\eqref{eq:zeroreduction_offset}}{=}\sum_{r^{\perp}\in\operatorname{kernel}(\alpha):\epsilon(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r^{\perp}_{0}))=g}\exp(q(\rho^{\perp}(r^{\perp}+r_{0}^{\perp}))+\log(|R|))\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:zeroreduction}}{=}\sum_{e\in E^{\prime}:\epsilon^{\prime}(e)=g}\exp(q^{\prime}(e))\overset{\eqref{eq:quadratic_tensor_definition}}{=}\mathcal{T}[E^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime},q^{\prime}](g)\;.\end{gathered}

(186)

For the equality labeled
*
, we have performed a change of summation variable from
r^{\perp}
to
r^{\perp}+r^{\perp}_{0}
.
We have used the exponential sum over group characters,

\sum_{r\in R}\exp(\chi(r))=|R|\delta_{\chi=0}\;,

(187)

for any abelian group
R
and
\chi\in R^{*}
.
Note that if
|R|
contains
\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}
factors, then we are missing factors of “
\infty
”.
This means that the resulting quadratic tensor is a
\delta
-distribution rather than the characteristic function of a subspace of zero measure.
∎

Reducibility —

The two types of reductions above reduce
E
(and also
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
) to some quotient group.
Let us now study to which extent
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
can be reduced by reductions.
We find that we can apply reductions until
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
only consists of
\mathbb{Z}
factors.

{myprop}

Assume that a quadratic tensor data cannot be further reduced using the three propositions above.
Then
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{n}
.

Proof.

Assume
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
contains an elementary abelian factor
A
that is equal to either
\mathbb{Z}_{k}
, or
\mathbb{R}
, or
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\alpha
be the injective homomorphism that embeds
A
into
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
.
We note that
\alpha^{+/*}q^{(2)}_{a/\phi}\alpha\in\hom[A|A^{+/*}]
and write
x_{a/\phi}\coloneqq\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{-1}(\alpha^{+/*}q_{a/\phi}^{(2)}\alpha)=(\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{2})^{-1}(q^{(2)}_{a/\phi}\circ(\alpha\times\alpha))
.

•

If
A=\mathbb{Z}_{k}
and
x_{\phi}\neq 0
, then let
R=\mathbb{Z}_{k/\gcd(k,x_{\phi})}
and
\rho=\alpha\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H(x_{\phi}\operatorname{mod}k/\gcd(k,x_{\phi}))\in\hom[R|E]
.
Then
\mathrlap{\raisebox{-1.72218pt}{$\overset{\sim}{\phantom{H}}$}}H^{-1}(\rho^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}\rho)=1
is invertible, and we can apply the reduction from Proposition
III.6
.

•

If
A=\mathbb{Z}_{k}
and
x_{\phi}=0
, we can apply the reduction from Proposition
III.6
.

•

If
A=\mathbb{R}
and
x_{\phi}\neq 0
or
x_{a}\neq 0
, then we can apply the reduction from Proposition
III.6
with
R=A
and
\rho=\alpha
.

•

If
A=\mathbb{R}
and
x_{\phi}=0
and
x_{a}=0
, then we can apply the reduction from Proposition
III.6
with
R=A
and
\rho=\alpha
.

•

If
A=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
then
x_{\phi}=0
and
x_{a}=0
automatically, and we can apply the reduction from Proposition
III.6
.

∎

On the other hand, if
A=\mathbb{Z}
, then
\alpha^{*}q_{\phi}^{(2)}\alpha\in\hom[\mathbb{Z}|\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}]
cannot have an inverse since there is only the trivial homomorphism from
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
to
\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus the reduction in Proposition
III.6
can never be applied for
R=\mathbb{Z}
.
Further, the reduction in Proposition
III.6
only applies to
R=\mathbb{R}
and not
R=\mathbb{Z}
.

Let us now provide some evidence that performing tensor network contractions is in general hard if
\operatorname{kernel}(\epsilon^{(1)})
contains a large number of
\mathbb{Z}
factors.
